Every node sum has absolute value at most $T(\xi)$, hence less than $K$. At a node with $t_a\ne0$, let $q$ be the number of its level primes dividing $t_a$. If $q>0$, distinctness and <a href="#eq:exc-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-primes">[eq:exc-primes]</a> give $$m^{3q}
 <\prod_{p_{\ell,i}\mid t_a}p_{\ell,i}
 \le |t_a|<m^{3h/4}.$$ Thus $q<h/4$; the same inequality is immediate when $q=0$. For every other child, <a href="#eq:exc-annihilator-congruence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-annihilator-congruence">[eq:exc-annihilator-congruence]</a> forces $t_{ai}\ne0$. Since $h=m$, each nonzero internal node has at least $3m/4$ nonzero children. Starting with the root, this gives at least $(3m/4)^h$ nonzero leaf coefficients. These are nonzero integers, so $$T(\xi)\ge(3m/4)^h\ge m^{3h/4}=K$$ for all sufficiently large $m$, a contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The distortion and length estimates

<a id="eq:exc-normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:exc-fourier-energy"></a>
Let $f$ be any injection of the finite set $X(G)$ into $\ell_1$, with distortion $D$. Rescale it by its contraction factor so that $$\label{eq:exc-normalization}
 \operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le\|f(x)-f(y)\|_1
       \le D\operatorname{ED}(x,y)
 \qquad(x,y\in X(G)).$$ This applies to each injection individually. Put $g(v)=f(X(v))/n_*$ and, using uniform measure on $G$, define $$\mathcal E(u)=\mathbb E_{v\in G}\|g(v+u)-g(v)\|_1 .$$ The cut–Fourier representation in Lemma <a href="#pre:weighted-circle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:weighted-circle">8</a> provides nonnegative weights $\omega_\xi$ such that $$\label{eq:exc-fourier-energy}
 \mathcal E(u)=\sum_\xi\omega_\xi|1-\chi_\xi(u)|^2 .$$ The lemma applies with period $L$, $m^h$ coordinates, subgroup $H$, and threshold $K$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:exc-annihilator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exc-annihilator">13</a>. Its representation and intermediate weight estimates use normalized counting measure and also apply when the target $\ell_1$ has infinitely many coordinates.

<a id="eq:exc-off-annihilator"></a>
We spell out the two resulting bounds on frequency mass. Let $\mathcal D_L=\{1,2,4,\ldots\}\cap[1,L/2]$. For every $s\in\mathcal D_L$, <a href="#eq:exc-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-normalization">[eq:exc-normalization]</a> and <a href="#eq:exc-binary-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-binary-coordinate">[eq:exc-binary-coordinate]</a> imply $$\frac Ls\sum_{a\in\Lambda}\mathcal E((s/L)e_a)
 \le \frac Ls\,m^h\frac{2D}{m^h}\frac{s}{L}=2D.$$ The weighted estimate in Lemma <a href="#pre:weighted-circle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pre:weighted-circle">8</a> therefore gives <a id="eq:exc-weighted-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:exc-weighted-mass}
 \begin{aligned}
 4\sum_\xi\omega_\xi T(\xi)
 &\le\sum_{s\in\mathcal D_L}\frac Ls
           \sum_{a\in\Lambda}\mathcal E((s/L)e_a)\\
 &\le 2D|\mathcal D_L|
 \le C D\log(L+1).
 \end{aligned}$$ In particular $\sum_\xi\omega_\xi T(\xi)\le C D\log(L+1)$ after adjusting the absolute constant. The subgroup identity in that lemma, together with <a href="#eq:exc-binary-subgroup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-binary-subgroup">[eq:exc-binary-subgroup]</a>, gives $$\label{eq:exc-off-annihilator}
 \sum_{\xi\notin H^\perp}\omega_\xi
 =\frac12\mathbb E_{u\in H}\mathcal E(u)
 \le \frac{Dh}{R}.$$ This is the uniform average on the finite subgroup $H$ itself.

<a id="eq:exc-final-energy"></a>
The half-turn multiplier in <a href="#eq:exc-fourier-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-fourier-energy">[eq:exc-fourier-energy]</a> equals four when $t_{\varnothing}$ is odd and zero otherwise. A detecting frequency inside $H^\perp$ has $T(\xi)\ge K$, whereas <a href="#eq:exc-off-annihilator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-off-annihilator">[eq:exc-off-annihilator]</a> controls all frequencies outside $H^\perp$. Using also the noncontracting half-turn bound from <a href="#eq:exc-binary-far" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-binary-far">[eq:exc-binary-far]</a>, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \eta3^{-h}F_h
 &\le \mathcal E(w_*) \notag\\
 &\le 4\sum_{\xi\notin H^\perp}\omega_\xi
       +\frac4K\sum_\xi\omega_\xi T(\xi) \notag\\
 &\le C D\left(\frac hR+\frac{\log(L+1)}K\right).
 \label{eq:exc-final-energy}
\end{aligned}$$ Thus the separation in the word metric and the branching of frequencies have now been compared using the same finite subgroup.

<a id="eq:exc-logL"></a>
<a id="eq:exc-distortion-power"></a>
The prime bounds give $$\label{eq:exc-logL}
 \log L\le\log2+5hm\log m.$$ With $h=m$, $R=m^{2h}$, and $K=m^{3h/4}$, the term $h/R$ in <a href="#eq:exc-final-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-final-energy">[eq:exc-final-energy]</a> is bounded by $\log(L+1)/K$ for all large $m$. Since $F_h=m^{-h/4-100m^{3/4}}$, it follows that <a id="eq:exc-distortion-explicit"></a>$$\label{eq:exc-distortion-explicit}
 D\ge c\eta\,
       \frac{3^{-m}m^{m/2-100m^{3/4}}}{\log(L+1)}$$ for an absolute $c>0$. The logarithm of the right side, divided by $m\log m$, tends to $1/2$: the losses contribute $100m^{-1/4}$, $\log3/\log m$, and a term tending to zero by <a href="#eq:exc-logL" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-logL">[eq:exc-logL]</a>. Therefore, after increasing an absolute threshold, $$\label{eq:exc-distortion-power}
 D\ge m^{m/4}\qquad(m\ge m_0).$$ Because $f$ was arbitrary, this is a distortion lower bound for the finite set $X(G)$.

<a id="eq:exc-binary-length"></a>
Finally, <a href="#eq:exc-size-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-size-parameters">[eq:exc-size-parameters]</a> and <a href="#eq:exc-length-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-length-recurrence">[eq:exc-length-recurrence]</a> give the exact root length $$N=L\prod_{\ell=1}^h(mRP_\ell)
   =\frac{L^2}{2}(mR)^h.$$ Consequently $$\log N\le12m^2\log m+m\log m+\log2.$$ Also $J=2Lm^h$ and $w\le C_{\rm code}\log(2J)$, so $\log w=O(\log m)$. Hence, for an absolute $C_0$, $$\label{eq:exc-binary-length}
 \log n_*=\log(wN)\le C_0m^2\log m
 \qquad(m\ge m_0).$$ We have obtained these binary same-length witnesses for every sufficiently large integer $m$. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">7</a> applies its direct all-length inversion to <a href="#eq:exc-distortion-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-distortion-power">[eq:exc-distortion-power]</a> and <a href="#eq:exc-binary-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-binary-length">[eq:exc-binary-length]</a>, yielding a witness of one length at most each sufficiently large cap.

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Every length cap and every finite alphabet

Each lower construction supplies binary words of one common length for every sufficiently large integer value of its parameter. We now choose that parameter directly from an arbitrary length cap. This avoids any need to compare successive witness lengths.

<a id="stmt-assembly-inversion"></a>
<div id="assembly:inversion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (All-length inversion). *Let $c_0,C_0>0$ be fixed. Suppose that for every sufficiently large integer $k$ there is an integer $n_k\geq1$ and a finite subset of $\{0,1\}^{n_k}$ whose least $\ell_1$ distortion is at least $\exp(c_0k\log k)$, where $\log n_k\leq C_0k^2\log k$. Then there are constants $c>0$ and $d_0$, depending only on $c_0,C_0$ and the starting threshold, such that for every integer $d\geq d_0$ a binary subset of one length at most $d$ has distortion at least $\exp(c\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-inversion"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-inversion</summary>

*Proof.* Put $t=\log d$ and choose a fixed $a>0$ with $C_0a^2\leq1/2$. Set $k=\lfloor a\sqrt{t/\log t}\rfloor$. For sufficiently large $t$, this is above the construction threshold and satisfies $k\geq(a/2)\sqrt{t/\log t}$, $\log k\geq(1/3)\log t$, and $k\leq t$. Therefore $$\log n_k\leq C_0k^2\log k\leq C_0a^2t\leq t/2<t,
 \qquad k\log k\geq(a/6)\sqrt{t\log t}.$$ The first inequality puts the witness below the cap; the second gives the asserted distortion. ◻

</details>

For the reset construction, use <a href="#mask:distortion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mask:distortion">[mask:distortion]</a> and <a href="#mask:length-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mask:length-bound">[mask:length-bound]</a> with $k=h$. For the excluded-coordinate construction, use <a href="#eq:exc-distortion-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-distortion-power">[eq:exc-distortion-power]</a> and <a href="#eq:exc-binary-length" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exc-binary-length">[eq:exc-binary-length]</a> with $k=m$. Each separately satisfies the lemma. Restricting a map on $\{0,1\}^{\leq d}$ to either hard subset proves the binary lower bound for every sufficiently large $d$.

A binary subalphabet is isometric for ordinary edit distance inside any larger alphabet. One inequality follows by using a binary edit script. For the reverse one, project all extra alphabet symbols to either binary symbol and apply this projection to an ambient edit script. Each edit projects to one edit or no change, while the binary endpoints remain fixed. Thus the same lower bound holds for every finite alphabet with at least two letters, with constants independent of its size.

Theorem <a href="#thm:uniform-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:uniform-upper">15</a> in the next section supplies the upper bound on the whole length-capped space, completing Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Its fresh-symbol padding serves a different purpose from the binary code transfers: the latter are applied only at equal lengths, while the upper map must compare every pair of shorter words simultaneously.

<a id="upper:section"></a>
### An alphabet-uniform embedding by substring histograms

We now construct an embedding on the whole set of words of bounded length. For a finite alphabet $\Sigma$, let $\Sigma^{\le d}$ include all words of length at most $d$, including the empty word. Write $\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$ for the minimum number of single-symbol insertions, deletions, and substitutions taking $x$ to $y$, each at unit cost. Intermediate words may have arbitrary length. All logarithms in this section are natural.

<a id="stmt-thm-uniform-upper"></a>
<div id="thm:uniform-upper" class="theorem">

**Theorem 15** (Uniform upper bound). *<span id="upper:uniform" label="upper:uniform"></span>There are absolute constants $C>0$ and $d_0\ge3$ such that, for every finite alphabet $\Sigma$ with $|\Sigma|\ge2$ and every integer $d\ge d_0$, there are an integer $M\ge1$ and a deterministic map $F:\Sigma^{\le d}\longrightarrow\ell_1^M$ satisfying $$\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le \|F(x)-F(y)\|_1
 \le \exp\!\bigl(C\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\bigr)
       \operatorname{ED}(x,y)
 \qquad(x,y\in\Sigma^{\le d}).$$ Here $\ell_1^M$ is real, and both $C$ and $d_0$ are independent of $\Sigma$.*

</div>

The construction develops the block-and-shift recursion of Ostrovsky and Rabani <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[OstrovskyRabani2007](#ref-OstrovskyRabani2007)]. They compare multisets of overlapping shifted substrings using embeddings of shorter strings, and their introduction also identifies extensions to larger alphabets and varying lengths. We give the estimates for these extensions explicitly.

The recursive step represents a block by lists of overlapping windows. After embedding the shorter windows, we record how many of them fall in each part of a random partition. Averaging over every partition outcome gives one deterministic $\ell_1$ map. A pair of distant blocks has a scale at which every cross-pair of windows is distant, so their histograms are often disjoint. For expansion, a deletion followed by an insertion shifts most blocks by one position, allowing most windows to be paired identically. These observations prove the theorem first at power lengths. We then construct a map using the actual window lengths and analyze its expansion both through an arbitrary longest common subsequence and through moves. A final variant uses total variation in the power-length recursion.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Subsequence deficits and local moves

A common subsequence is an increasing matching of equal symbols; its maximum size is $\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)$. For arbitrary words put $$q(x,y)=\max(|x|,|y|)-\operatorname{LCS}(x,y),$$ and for words of the same length $n$ put $r_n(x,y)=n-\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)$. Thus $r_n$ counts the unmatched positions on either one side of a longest common subsequence.

<a id="stmt-upper-metric"></a>
<div id="upper:metric" class="lemma">

<a id="upper:unequal-comparison"></a>
<a id="upper:deficit-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Metric comparisons). *Let $\Delta(x,y)$ be the minimum number of insertions and deletions when substitutions are forbidden. For all finite words, $$\Delta(x,y)=|x|+|y|-2\operatorname{LCS}(x,y),\qquad
 \operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le\Delta(x,y)\le2\operatorname{ED}(x,y),$$ and $$\label{upper:unequal-comparison}
 q(x,y)\le\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le2q(x,y).$$ In particular, for $|x|=|y|=n$, $$\label{upper:deficit-comparison}
 r_n(x,y)=\tfrac12\Delta(x,y),\qquad
 r_n(x,y)\le\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le2r_n(x,y).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-metric"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-metric</summary>

*Proof.* In a script of $k$ edits, at least $|x|-k$ original symbols survive unchanged and in order. Applying this also to the reverse script gives $q(x,y)\le k$. Deleting the letters outside a longest common subsequence and inserting the missing target letters uses $|x|+|y|-2\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)\le2q(x,y)$ edits. Conversely, the survivors of any insertion–deletion script form a common subsequence, so that script has at least this many edits. This proves the formula for $\Delta$. Ordinary edits allow insertions and deletions, while replacing each substitution by one deletion and one insertion proves the factor-two comparison. These alignment identities are the insertion–deletion specialization of the trace formulation of Wagner and Fischer <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[WagnerFischer1974](#ref-WagnerFischer1974), Sections 3 and 5]. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-upper-move"></a>
<div id="upper:move" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Moves and their restrictions). *Two words of length $n\ge0$ can be joined by exactly $r_n(x,y)$ moves, each deleting one symbol and inserting one symbol while preserving length $n$. For a single move, restrictions to any common interval of $a$ positions have deficit at most one, and hence edit distance at most two.*

*For a fixed partition of the positions into nonempty consecutive blocks, at most two blocks contain a deletion or insertion endpoint. In any other block of length $m$, the two subwords agree, or the prefix of length $m-1$ of one agrees with the suffix of length $m-1$ of the other.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-move"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-move</summary>

*Proof.* Protect the occurrences of a longest common subsequence in $x$, labeling them by their positions in $y$. Delete an unprotected symbol and insert a missing target symbol in its prescribed order among the protected ones, then protect it. After $r_n(x,y)$ repetitions every target position is protected, and the word is $y$.

For one move, let $p$ be its deleted position in $x$ and $q$ its inserted position in $y$. If $p<q$, positions outside $[p,q]$ match identically, while positions $p+1,\ldots,q$ of $x$ match positions $p,\ldots,q-1$ of $y$. Restricting this matching to the same interval on both sides loses at most one position on each side, so its size is at least $a-1$. Interchanging the words handles $p>q$; when $p=q$ only one position changes. The same description shows that a block avoiding both endpoints is unchanged or shifted by one position, in the precise sense stated. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-upper-padding"></a>
<div id="upper:padding" class="lemma">

<a id="upper:common-suffix"></a>
<a id="upper:fresh-padding"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Common and fresh suffixes). *For equal-length words $u,v$ of length $m$ and any common suffix $z$, $$\label{upper:common-suffix}
 r_{m+|z|}(uz,vz)=r_m(u,v).$$ If $\ast$ occurs in neither $x$ nor $y$, and $N\ge\max(|x|,|y|)$, then, writing $\widehat x=x\ast^{N-|x|}$ and $\widehat y=y\ast^{N-|y|}$, $$\label{upper:fresh-padding}
 \operatorname{LCS}(\widehat x,\widehat y)
 =\operatorname{LCS}(x,y)+N-\max(|x|,|y|),\qquad
 r_N(\widehat x,\widehat y)=q(x,y).$$ The second identity includes empty inputs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-padding"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-padding</summary>

*Proof.* An optimal subsequence matching of two words ending in the same symbol can be chosen to match their last positions. If an optimal matching uses either last position, its last such pair can be replaced by the pair of last positions; if it uses neither, that pair can be appended. Removing the final pair shows $\operatorname{LCS}(ua,va)\le1+\operatorname{LCS}(u,v)$. Appending the pair to an optimal matching of the prefixes proves the reverse inequality. Iteration over $z$ proves <a href="#upper:common-suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:common-suffix">[upper:common-suffix]</a>.

For fresh-symbol padding, matched original symbols form a common subsequence of $x,y$, and matched $\ast$ symbols form a final part of size at most $\min(N-|x|,N-|y|)$. These two maxima are attained consecutively. Their sum gives <a href="#upper:fresh-padding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:fresh-padding">[upper:fresh-padding]</a>. ◻

</details>

The recursion at equal lengths will use a common suffix with arbitrary symbols. The final passage to varying lengths will use the fresh symbol in <a href="#upper:fresh-padding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:fresh-padding">[upper:fresh-padding]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### A finite histogram component

The next construction turns a distance between shorter words into a distance between lists of such words. We use the standard cut representation of finite $\ell_1$ metrics <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[LinialLondonRabinovich1995](#ref-LinialLondonRabinovich1995), Section 4]. The proof below includes the summability needed when the original target has infinitely many coordinates.

<a id="stmt-upper-partitions"></a>
<div id="upper:partitions" class="lemma">

<a id="upper:collision"></a>
<a id="upper:histogram-map"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Finite random partitions). *Let $X$ be finite and $\rho(u,v)=\|f(u)-f(v)\|_1$ for a map $f:X\to\ell_1$ into real sequence space. For every $\alpha>0$ there is a probability distribution with finite support on partitions $\pi$ of $X$ such that $$\label{upper:collision}
 \Pr[\pi(u)=\pi(v)]=e^{-\alpha\rho(u,v)}.$$ Here $\pi(u)$ denotes the part containing $u$. For a finite list $L$ in $X$, let $H_L^\pi$ be its count vector on the parts of $\pi$, with repetitions counted. Then $$\label{upper:histogram-map}
 \mathcal H_\alpha(L)=\bigoplus_\pi \Pr[\pi]\,H_L^\pi$$ is a deterministic finite-dimensional map whose distance equals $\mathbb E\|H_L^\pi-H_{L'}^\pi\|_1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-partitions"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-partitions</summary>

*Proof.* For real $a,b$, $|a-b|=\int_{\mathbb R}|\mathbf 1_{a>t}-\mathbf 1_{b>t}|\,dt$. Apply this to every coordinate of $f$ and group the threshold cuts by the subset of $X$ they select. After discarding the empty and full cuts, $$\rho(u,v)=\sum_{\varnothing\ne A\subsetneq X}
 a_A|\mathbf 1_A(u)-\mathbf 1_A(v)|,\qquad a_A\ge0.$$ Every nontrivial cut separates some unordered pair. Consequently $\sum_Aa_A\le\sum_{\{u,v\}\subset X}\rho(u,v)<\infty$. This also justifies the grouping when $f$ has infinitely many coordinates; all sums and integrals are nonnegative, and there are only finitely many subsets of $X$.

Select each cut $A$ independently with probability $1-e^{-\alpha a_A}$, and partition $X$ by the membership bits in the selected cuts. Points $u,v$ share a part exactly when no separating cut is selected. Taking the product of these probabilities gives <a href="#upper:collision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:collision">[upper:collision]</a>. There are finitely many cut selections. Finally, additivity of the $\ell_1$ norm on disjoint coordinates proves the distance identity for <a href="#upper:histogram-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:histogram-map">[upper:histogram-map]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-upper-histogram"></a>
<div id="upper:histogram" class="lemma">

<a id="upper:histogram-lower"></a>
<a id="upper:coupling"></a>
<a id="upper:normalizations"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Histogram estimates). *Let $s\ge1$ be an integer and let $L,L'$ be lists of size $s$ in the set of Lemma <a href="#upper:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:partitions">19</a>, and set $T=\mathbb E\|H_L^\pi-H_{L'}^\pi\|_1$. If every cross-pair has $\rho$-distance at least $a$, then $$\label{upper:histogram-lower}
 T\ge2s(1-s^2e^{-\alpha a}).$$ If a pairing of list occurrences leaves $t$ occurrences unpaired on each side, then $$\label{upper:coupling}
 T\le2t+2\sum_{(u,v)\ {\rm paired}}(1-e^{-\alpha\rho(u,v)})
   \le2t+2\alpha\sum_{(u,v)\ {\rm paired}}\rho(u,v).$$ Writing $P_L^\pi=H_L^\pi/s$ and $\operatorname{TV}(P,Q)=\tfrac12\|P-Q\|_1$, the exact normalizations are $$\label{upper:normalizations}
 \|H_L^\pi-H_{L'}^\pi\|_1
 =s\|P_L^\pi-P_{L'}^\pi\|_1
 =2s\,\operatorname{TV}(P_L^\pi,P_{L'}^\pi).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-histogram"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-histogram</summary>

*Proof.* The probability of any cross-collision is at most $s^2e^{-\alpha a}$ by a union bound. With no cross-collision the two count vectors have disjoint supports and mass $s$ each, hence distance $2s$. This proves <a href="#upper:histogram-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:histogram-lower">[upper:histogram-lower]</a>. For the upper bound, each unpaired occurrence costs one in the triangle inequality. A paired pair costs two if its parts differ and zero otherwise. Take expectations using <a href="#upper:collision" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:collision">[upper:collision]</a> and then use $1-e^{-z}\le z$ for $z\ge0$. The normalization identities follow from the definitions. ◻

</details>

Thus a lower estimate for one component follows from separation of all cross-pairs, while an upper estimate follows from a pairing of occurrences. Both estimates concern the same map <a href="#upper:histogram-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:histogram-map">[upper:histogram-map]</a>, whose partition distribution is fixed on the full finite domain before any pair of input words is considered.

<a id="upper:powers"></a>
#### Power lengths and count histograms

<details id="proof-stmt-upper-histogram-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-upper-histogram</summary>

<a id="upper:power-normalization"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#upper:uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:uniform">[upper:uniform]</a>.* Fix integers $b\ge2$ and $H\ge1$, put $n=b^H$, and fix a nonempty finite alphabet $\Gamma$. We construct maps $F_k$ at lengths $b^k$, $0\le k\le H$, with $$\label{upper:power-normalization}
 r_{b^k}(x,y)\le\|F_k(x)-F_k(y)\|_1
             \le\Lambda_k r_{b^k}(x,y).$$ At length one, $a\mapsto\tfrac12e_a$ has distance exactly $r_1$, so $\Lambda_0=1$.

Index positions from zero and write $x_{[a,b)}$ for the subword in positions $a,\ldots,b-1$. Suppose $1\le k\le H$, set $m=b^{k-1}$ and $N=bm$, and assume <a href="#upper:power-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:power-normalization">[upper:power-normalization]</a> at level $k-1$. Split each length-$N$ word into $b$ consecutive blocks of length $m$. At each dyadic scale $s\le m$, block $j$ supplies the list $$W_{j,s}(x)=
 \bigl(x_{[jm+r,\,jm+r+m-s+1)}\bigr)_{r=0}^{s-1},
 \qquad 0\le j<b.$$ These are its $s$ windows of length $m-s+1$, counted with multiplicity. Choose one fixed suffix $z_s\in\Gamma^{s-1}$ and embed a window $u$ by $F_{k-1}(uz_s)$. By <a href="#upper:common-suffix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:common-suffix">[upper:common-suffix]</a>, the resulting distance $\rho_s$ satisfies $$r_{m-s+1}(u,v)\le\rho_s(u,v)
       \le\Lambda_{k-1}r_{m-s+1}(u,v).$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#upper:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:partitions">19</a> on the full domain $\Gamma^{m-s+1}$ at rate <a id="upper:lambda-local"></a>$$\label{upper:lambda-local}
 \alpha_s=\frac{4\log(s+1)}s.$$ Let $T_{j,s}(x,y)$ be the expected count-histogram distance of the two lists, and let $T_{k,s}=\sum_jT_{j,s}$. Define $F_k$ as four times the direct sum of these histogram maps over $j$ and $s$. Thus $$\|F_k(x)-F_k(y)\|_1=4\sum_sT_{k,s}(x,y).$$ Every map in this construction is finite-dimensional, since each level takes a finite direct sum of the maps in Lemma <a href="#upper:partitions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:partitions">19</a>.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Separation.

<a id="upper:power-scale"></a>
Suppose corresponding blocks have deficit $q>0$. The largest dyadic integer $s\le(q+1)/2$ is available and satisfies $$\label{upper:power-scale}
 \frac{q+1}{4}<s\le\frac{q+1}{2},\qquad s\ge\frac q4.$$ Every cross-pair $u,v$ of their windows satisfies $$r_{m-s+1}(u,v)
 \ge(m-s+1)-\operatorname{LCS}(\text{the two full blocks})
 =q-s+1\ge s.$$ The union bound in <a href="#upper:histogram-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:histogram-lower">[upper:histogram-lower]</a> is therefore at most $s^2/(s+1)^4\le1/2$, and this component is at least $s\ge q/4$. Common subsequences chosen separately in the blocks concatenate, so the sum of their deficits is at least $r_N(x,y)$. It follows that $\|F_k(x)-F_k(y)\|_1\ge r_N(x,y)$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Expansion along a move.

<a id="upper:power-recurrence"></a>
In a nonexceptional block from Lemma <a href="#upper:move" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:move">17</a>, the two window lists have at least $s-1$ identical occurrences. For a shifted block, pair starts displaced by one; for an unchanged block, pair equal starts. Equation <a href="#upper:coupling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:coupling">[upper:coupling]</a> bounds its contribution by $2$. In an exceptional block, pair windows at the same starts. Their deficits are at most one by Lemma <a href="#upper:move" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:move">17</a>, including after the common suffix is appended, so one such block costs at most $2s\alpha_s\Lambda_{k-1}=8\log(s+1)\Lambda_{k-1}$. There are at most two exceptional blocks. At every scale, <a id="upper:power-move"></a>$$\label{upper:power-move}
 T_{k,s}(x,y)\le2b+16\log(s+1)\Lambda_{k-1}
 \quad\text{for one move.}$$ There are at most $1+\lfloor\log_2m\rfloor$ scales. Summing this estimate, applying the factor $4$, and following the $r_N(x,y)$-move path gives $$\label{upper:power-recurrence}
 \Lambda_k\le C_1\log^2(n+2)(b+\Lambda_{k-1}),\qquad
 \Lambda_H\le b\bigl(C_2\log^2(n+2)\bigr)^H$$ with absolute constants. Indeed, after enlarging $A=C_1\log^2(n+2)$ so that $A\ge2$, induction gives $\Lambda_k\le b(2A)^k$.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Parameters and varying lengths.

<a id="upper:parameters"></a>
For sufficiently large $d$, take $$\label{upper:parameters}
 H=\left\lfloor\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{\log\log d}}\right\rfloor,\qquad
 b=\left\lceil d^{1/H}\right\rceil,\qquad n=b^H.$$ Here $d\le n$ and, since $b\le2d^{1/H}$, $\log n\le\log d+H\log2=O(\log d)$. Also $H$ has order $\sqrt{\log d/\log\log d}$, so $$\log\Lambda_H
 \le\log b+H\log\!\bigl(C_2\log^2(n+2)\bigr)
 =O\!\bigl(\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d}\bigr).$$ All constants are absolute. Work over $\Gamma=\Sigma\cup\{\ast\}$ with $\ast\notin\Sigma$ and append $\ast$ to every input until its length is $n$. By <a href="#upper:unequal-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:unequal-comparison">[upper:unequal-comparison]</a> and <a href="#upper:fresh-padding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:fresh-padding">[upper:fresh-padding]</a>, $F(x)=2F_H(\widehat x)$ satisfies $$\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le\|F(x)-F(y)\|_1
 \le2\Lambda_H\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$ Absorbing the factor two into the absolute exponential constant proves the theorem, including the empty word. ◻

</details>

<a id="upper:variants"></a>
#### Actual window lengths and two expansion estimates

<span id="upper:aligned" label="upper:aligned"></span>

The power-length construction padded each window to the preceding power. We now use maps at the actual window lengths. This gives one recursion whose expansion can be proved in two ways: directly from an arbitrary longest common subsequence, or from the move estimate already used above. Both arguments analyze the same deterministic histogram map.

<a id="upper:aligned-rate"></a>
Fix an integer cap $d\ge2$, an integer $B\ge2$, and a nonempty finite alphabet $\Gamma$. Construct maps $\Phi_n$ on $\Gamma^n$, $1\le n\le d$, whose distances lie between $\operatorname{ED}$ and $E_n\operatorname{ED}$, starting with $\Phi_1(a)=\tfrac12e_a$ and $E_1=1$. For $n>1$, partition the positions into $b_n=\min(B,n)$ nonempty blocks of nearly equal lengths, with boundaries $$0=p_0<p_1<\cdots<p_{b_n}=n,\qquad
 \ell_j=p_{j+1}-p_j\le\lceil n/B\rceil.$$ For each dyadic $s\le\ell_j$, use the list of $s$ windows $x_{[p_j+r,\,p_j+r+\ell_j-s+1)}$, $0\le r<s$. Embed each window by the map at its actual length. Writing $$D'=\max_{1\le t\le\lceil n/B\rceil}E_t,$$ its embedded distance satisfies $\operatorname{ED}\le\rho\le D'\operatorname{ED}$. Every child length is strictly less than $n$. Use count histograms at rate $$\label{upper:aligned-rate}
 \alpha_s=\lambda/s,\qquad \lambda=4\log(2d),$$ and define $\Phi_n$ as six times their direct sum. Write $T_{j,s}$ for the expected count distance. Equivalently, each component before the factor six is $s$ times the expected $\ell_1$ distance of probability histograms, by <a href="#upper:normalizations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:normalizations">[upper:normalizations]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Separation.

If corresponding blocks have edit distance $k_j>0$, then $k_j\le\ell_j$ by substitution. The largest dyadic $s\le(k_j+2)/3$ is available and satisfies $s\ge k_j/6$ and $k_j-2(s-1)\ge s$. Deleting the positions omitted from the two windows shows that every cross-window edit distance is at least $k_j-2(s-1)$. The probability of any cross-collision is at most $d^2e^{-\lambda}\le1/2$, so $T_{j,s}\ge s\ge k_j/6$. Blockwise edit scripts concatenate; hence the sum of the block distances dominates the whole-word distance. The factor six makes $\Phi_n$ noncontracting.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### Corresponding segments in an arbitrary alignment.

Fix an arbitrary longest common subsequence matching of $x,y$, of size $n-k$, where $k=r_n(x,y)$. Let $t_j$ be the number of matched source positions strictly before $p_j$. Choose target boundaries $$0=q_0\le q_1\le\cdots\le q_{b_n}=n$$ so that exactly $t_j$ matched target positions precede $q_j$. For an internal boundary, take $q_j$ to be the position of target match number $t_j+1$ if it exists, and take $q_j=n$ otherwise. This gives nondecreasing boundaries and permits repeated boundaries and empty target segments. The matching pairs exactly the matched positions of $[p_j,p_{j+1})$ with those of $[q_j,q_{j+1})$.

<a id="upper:boundary-shift"></a>
<a id="upper:unmatched-total"></a>
Let $A_j,B_j$ be the numbers of unmatched source and target positions in the prefixes ending at these boundaries. Since $p_j=t_j+A_j$, $q_j=t_j+B_j$, and $0\le A_j,B_j\le k$, $$\label{upper:boundary-shift}
 |q_j-p_j|\le k.$$ Let $a_j,b_j$ count unmatched positions in the source block and its aligned target segment, and put $e_j=a_j+b_j$. These segments partition the two words, so $$\label{upper:unmatched-total}
 \sum_j e_j=2k.$$ In a matched pair belonging to these segments, the relative indices measured from $p_j,q_j$ differ by at most $e_j$: their numbers of preceding matched positions agree, and their preceding unmatched counts are bounded by $a_j,b_j$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### Pairing the original window lists.

Put $L=\ell_j-s+1$. Pair the source window starting at $p_j+r$ with the target window starting at $q_j+r$ precisely when both belong to the original fixed-block lists. Thus $$0\le r<s,\qquad p_j\le q_j+r<p_j+s.$$ The two intervals of allowed starts overlap in $\max(0,s-|q_j-p_j|)$ positions. Exactly $\min(s,|q_j-p_j|)$ windows on each side remain unpaired.

Consider a paired window. At most $a_j\le e_j$ of its source positions are unmatched. For any other source position, its partner’s relative index differs by at most $e_j$. Therefore only matched source positions within $e_j$ positions of an endpoint can have partners outside the target window; there are at most $2e_j$ of them. The windows have a common subsequence of length at least $\max(0,L-3e_j)$, and <a href="#upper:deficit-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:deficit-comparison">[upper:deficit-comparison]</a> gives <a id="upper:aligned-window"></a>$$\label{upper:aligned-window}
 \operatorname{ED}(\text{paired windows})\le6e_j.$$ This reasoning also covers a target window extending outside its aligned segment. Every partner of a matched source-block position lies in that segment, and the argument counts precisely those partners that remain inside the target window. It requires no condition on the other target positions.

<a id="upper:aligned-component"></a>
Using the partial pairing in <a href="#upper:coupling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:coupling">[upper:coupling]</a>, the shorter-map expansion, and <a href="#upper:aligned-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:aligned-rate">[upper:aligned-rate]</a>, we obtain $$\label{upper:aligned-component}
 T_{j,s}(x,y)
 \le2|q_j-p_j|+2s\frac{\lambda}{s}D'(6e_j)
 =2|q_j-p_j|+12\lambda D'e_j.$$ Each block has at most $L_d=1+\lfloor\log_2d\rfloor$ scales. Summing <a href="#upper:aligned-component" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:aligned-component">[upper:aligned-component]</a>, using <a href="#upper:boundary-shift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:boundary-shift">[upper:boundary-shift]</a> and <a href="#upper:unmatched-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:unmatched-total">[upper:unmatched-total]</a>, and applying the factor six gives $$\|\Phi_n(x)-\Phi_n(y)\|_1
 \le6L_d(2Bk+24\lambda D'k)
 \le C_6\log^2(d+1)(B+D')\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$ The last inequality uses $k=r_n(x,y)\le\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$. The chosen alignment serves only to analyze two images; every partition and coordinate of $\Phi_n$ was fixed on the entire domain beforehand.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### An alternative expansion estimate from moves.

For a single move, a nonexceptional block has $s-1$ identical windows and therefore contributes at most two. In either of the at most two exceptional blocks, windows at equal starts have edit distance at most two. Their embedded distance is at most $2D'$, so <a href="#upper:coupling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:coupling">[upper:coupling]</a> gives a block cost at most $4s\alpha_sD'=4\lambda D'$. Thus, at each scale, <a id="upper:ed-move"></a>$$\label{upper:ed-move}
 \sum_jT_{j,s}(x,y)\le2B+8\lambda D'
 =2B+32\log(2d)D'.$$ Summing scales, multiplying by six, and following the $r_n(x,y)\le\operatorname{ED}(x,y)$ moves gives the alternative expansion estimate <a id="upper:ed-move-recurrence"></a>$$\label{upper:ed-move-recurrence}
 E_n\le C_3\log(2d)\bigl(B+\log(2d)D'\bigr).$$ The arbitrary-alignment argument established this order of expansion without decomposing the comparison into moves.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### Depth and varying lengths.

Both estimates have the form $E_n\le A(B+D')$ with $A=C\log^2(2d)$ for an absolute $C$. The identity <a id="upper:ceiling-identity"></a>$$\label{upper:ceiling-identity}
 \left\lceil\frac{\lceil n/B\rceil}{B}\right\rceil
 =\left\lceil\frac{n}{B^2}\right\rceil$$ follows because both sides are the least integer $t$ with $n\le B^2t$. It bounds the recursion depth by $h=\lceil\log_Bd\rceil$. The induction used in <a href="#upper:power-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:power-recurrence">[upper:power-recurrence]</a> gives expansion at most <a id="upper:ceiling-recurrence"></a>$$\label{upper:ceiling-recurrence}
 B\bigl(C_7\log^2(2d)\bigr)^h.$$ For sufficiently large $d$, take $B=\lceil\exp(\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})\rceil$. The logarithm of this bound is then $O(\sqrt{\log d\,\log\log d})$.

Finally, work over $\Gamma=\Sigma\cup\{\ast\}$ and append the fresh symbol to length $d$. Erasing $\ast$ from a padded edit script projects each operation to at most one edit, and <a href="#upper:deficit-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:deficit-comparison">[upper:deficit-comparison]</a> and <a href="#upper:fresh-padding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:fresh-padding">[upper:fresh-padding]</a> give $$\operatorname{ED}(x,y)\le\operatorname{ED}(\widehat x,\widehat y)
 \le2r_d(\widehat x,\widehat y)=2q(x,y)
 \le2\operatorname{ED}(x,y).$$ Thus $\Phi_d(\widehat x)$ proves Theorem <a href="#upper:uniform" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:uniform">[upper:uniform]</a> on all shorter words, including the empty word, with an additional expansion factor two.

<a id="upper:tv"></a>
#### Total-variation normalization

A final variant retains the power lengths $n_i=b^i$ and the common-suffix window embeddings of Section <a href="#upper:powers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upper:powers">8.3</a>. Construct $V_i$ with distances between $r_{n_i}$ and $D_i r_{n_i}$, starting with $V_0(a)=\tfrac12e_a$ and $D_0=1$. At scale $s$, use rate <a id="upper:tv-rate"></a>$$\label{upper:tv-rate}
 \alpha_s=\frac{\log(2s^2)}s$$ and $s$ times expected total variation of probability histograms. This component is half the count distance. For block deficit $q>0$, choose $s$ as in <a href="#upper:power-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:power-scale">[upper:power-scale]</a>. Every cross-window deficit is at least $s$, so the probability of any cross-collision is at most $s^2e^{-\log(2s^2)}=1/2$. The component is at least $s/2>q/8$; eight times the summed map is therefore noncontracting for $r_{n_i}$.

Along a move, a nonexceptional block costs at most one, while an exceptional block costs at most $s\alpha_sD_{i-1}$. Consequently the unscaled cost at each scale is at most <a id="upper:tv-move"></a>$$\label{upper:tv-move}
 b+2\log(2s^2)D_{i-1},$$ and the move path gives <a id="upper:tv-recurrence"></a>$$\label{upper:tv-recurrence}
 D_i\le C_5\log(2n_i)\bigl(b+\log(2n_i)D_{i-1}\bigr).$$ Choose $b,H,n$ as in <a href="#upper:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:parameters">[upper:parameters]</a>. Bounding the logarithmic factors by those at $n=b^H$ and using <a href="#upper:power-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upper:power-recurrence">[upper:power-recurrence]</a> yields $D_H\le b(C\log^2(2n))^H$ for an absolute $C$. The parameter calculation in the power-length proof gives the required exponent. Its fresh-symbol padding and final multiplication by two apply to $V_H$, proving the same theorem on all shorter words.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Two consecutive matches from one source row enter different target rows. The labels are child tags. The open target interval between the arrows is unmatched and contains at least $(t−1)L$ positions. The drawing shows $t = 5$ schematically; only the two relevant matches are shown.",
      "figure_id": "fig:split-row",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:split-row",
          "exact_quote": "Two consecutive matches from one source row enter different target\nrows. The labels are child tags. The open target interval between the arrows\nis unmatched and contains at least $(t-1)L$ positions. The drawing shows\n$t=5$ schematically; on",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=.72cm,y=.75cm,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-.25,2) {source row};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-.25,0) {target rows};\n \\foreach \\i in {0,...,4} {\n   \\draw[fill=blue!5] (\\i,1.65) rectangle ++(1,.7);\n   \\pgfmathtruncatemacro{\\tag}{\\i+1}\n   \\node at (\\i+.5,2) {$\\tag$};\n }\n \\foreach \\i in {0,...,9} {\n   \\draw[fill=black!3] (\\i,-.35) rectangle ++(1,.7);\n   \\pgfmathtruncatemacro{\\tag}{mod(\\i,5)+1}\n   \\node at (\\i+.5,0) {$\\tag$};\n }\n \\draw[very thick] (5,-.5)--(5,.5);\n \\draw[->,thick,blue!60!black] (.5,1.6)--(.5,.4);\n \\draw[->,thick,blue!60!black] (3.5,1.6)--(8.5,.4);\n \\draw[<->] (.5,-.8)--(8.5,-.8);\n \\node[fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (4.5,-.8)\n   {unmatched gap, length at least $(t-1)L$};\n \\node at (2.5,-1.4) {one row};\n \\node at (7.5,-1.4) {next row};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic matching diagram between a source row and target rows. At the top, \"source row\" consists of five adjacent boxed cells labeled 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. Below, \"target rows\" consists of two consecutive rows of five boxed cells each, labeled 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, with braces underneath labeling the first as \"one row\" and the second as \"next row\". Two blue directed arrows show matches from the source row to target rows: the first arrow goes from cell 1 of the source row to cell 1 of the first target row; the second arrow goes from cell 2 of the source row to cell 5 of the second target row. A horizontal brace underneath the target rows spans from cell 2 of the first target row to cell 4 of the second target row, labeled \"unmatched gap, length at least $(t - 1)L$\"."
    }
  ],
